## Guest TLS verification latency: 2026-10-13

The stage trace in https-stage-diagnostic.log completed the TLS handshake at 14.34 seconds, then repeated certificate-chain verification until 25.86 seconds.  The first HTTP write failed immediately afterward.  A second trace recorded ESTABLISHED before the handshake's chain verification at 13.35 seconds and CLOSE-WAIT when it returned at 64.8 seconds.  Its attempt to send Client Finished then failed.  Evidence is https-stage-state-diagnostic.log.  The first version of that diagnostic had an invalid function parameter list from a text replacement and was corrected before running.  Both diagnostic runs restored the temporarily wrapped functions.

The peer therefore closes during certificate processing in the second run.  The ASDF rebuild is concurrently compiling Ironclad.  Timing must be repeated after it completes before attributing the remaining latency to a particular implementation.

Source review found two chain verifications with the same certificates, trust roots, server-auth purpose, and context depth: PROCESS-CERTIFICATE-VERIFY during the handshake and MAKE-TLS-CLIENT-STREAM afterward.  The recommendation is to retain the handshake verification and remove the repeated chain verification in the stream constructor.  The constructor must retain its explicit hostname check, because SNI-HOSTNAME can differ from HOSTNAME, and retain recording of the verified hostname for session tickets.  Certificate signatures, trust anchors, validity dates, purpose, and depth remain checked during the handshake.  No trust decision is cached or bypassed.  This removes duplicate cryptographic work but does not establish that the guest will meet the peer's timeout.

Approval is pending for that TLS verification change.  Tests must confirm one chain verification for a full handshake, success with the trusted bundle, rejection of an empty bundle and a mismatched verification hostname with correct SNI, and successful HTTP exchange under SBCL and Mezzano.  Authenticated guest inference remains untested.

The product HTTPS reference now documents the Responses API, CA preparation, errors, credential persistence, and current guest failure.  It states that cancellation closes the socket but does not interrupt CPU-bound certificate verification or guarantee a return deadline.  The Document Examiner catalog and tests include that reference.  Guest compilation and catalog loading passed, followed by 16 books, 123 topics, and 95 references with structure, search, missing-topic handling, command, Unicode cursor, and session-cleanup tests.  Evidence is https-examiner-load.log and https-examiner-test.log.

[RFC 8446 section 4.4.2.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8446.html#section-4.4.2.4) specifies TLS-specific certificate handling and refers detailed certificate validation to RFC 5280.  The proposed change retains pure-tls's existing handshake validation.  It remains pending approval.

The user approved removal of the duplicate chain verification.  The constructor will retain its hostname check and verified-hostname recording.  The transport test will count chain verification calls during its successful full handshake while executing the original verifier, then require exactly one call.  Existing empty-trust and mismatched-hostname tests will retain their checks.  The count instrumentation exists only during that test and is removed on exit.

The corrected SBCL transport test passed exactly one chain verification, both certificate-rejection cases, HTTP 401 JSON parsing, and the HTTP cleanup fixture in https-single-verification-host.log.  The preceding full guest rebuild completed in https-runtime-retained-status.log.  The guest then compiled the corrected TLS sources and passed entropy, atomics, cancellation, HTTP cleanup, the single-verification assertion, and both certificate-rejection cases.  Its GET stopped during TLS close-notify after Drakma had set DONE to T.  Evidence is https-single-retained-status.log, https-single-inspect.log, and https-single-locals.log.  That test was aborted after inspection.

A separate trace completed a resumed TLS handshake at 1.30 seconds and entered normal TLS CLOSE at 1.63 seconds with TCP state CLOSE-WAIT.  The first close-notify write signaled CONNECTION-CLOSED before cleanup.  Evidence is https-tls-close-diagnostic.log.  The earlier duplicate verification and compilation load affected handshake latency.  The current failure is the TCP send path's explicit rejection of CLOSE-WAIT.

## TCP half-close proposal: 2026-10-13

[RFC 9293 sections 3.6.1 and 3.10.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9293.html#section-3.6.1) permit sending after the peer has closed its sending direction.  Mezzano enters CLOSE-WAIT on peer FIN, but TCP-SEND accepts only ESTABLISHED.  Its CLOSE-WAIT receive branch also discards acknowledgments.  LAST-ACK currently removes the connection on any ACK, which can discard outstanding data when a late data acknowledgment arrives before the FIN acknowledgment.

The recommendation is to support sending in CLOSE-WAIT, reuse the established-state acknowledgment processing for that state, and retain outstanding data until acknowledged.  Local FIN must consume its sequence number, and closing-state transitions must require acknowledgment of that FIN.  The stream must remain open for output after receive EOF.  This supports TLS close-notify through the existing TCP implementation.  Errors will continue to propagate.

Tests must cover receive EOF followed by successful transmission, acknowledgment and retransmission-queue cleanup, a data-only ACK before the FIN ACK, FIN with duplicate ACK and buffered data, sequence wraparound, ordinary close, abort, and the HTTPS transport checks.  Kernel installation will use the established wired-code policy.  A fresh boot and snapshot restoration remain required.  Approval is pending for this TCP behavior change.

The user approved the TCP half-close correction.  The full-handshake count test now binds an empty session-ticket cache for that handshake so a previous test's ticket cannot turn the counted operation into session resumption.  The other transport checks retain their normal cache behavior.

## TLS session resumption authentication: 2026-10-13

Repeating the host transport tests with a populated session-ticket cache accepted a mismatched verification hostname.  A separate run also accepted an empty trust store.  Both checks fail in https-resumption-before.log.  The full-handshake tests reject both cases.  The cache is keyed only by the SNI hostname, and resumed handshakes recover only a previously verified hostname.  The constructor skips its explicit hostname check because the resumed handshake has no peer certificate.  The resumed handshake also omits verification against the current trust store.

The recommendation is to retain the peer certificate chain in each session ticket and validate that chain against the current verification mode, trust store, validity time, purpose, and depth when resuming.  The resumed handshake will restore its peer certificate and chain, allowing the constructor's existing requested-hostname check to run.  A shared handshake helper will perform chain and handshake-hostname validation for both full and resumed handshakes.  Each connection will verify its chain once.  Existing cached tickets lacking certificates must fail required verification.  This uses the existing verifier and avoids another cache-identity scheme.  Tests must populate the cache, confirm resumption of a valid session, then require rejection of a mismatched requested hostname and an empty trust store.  Approval is pending.

## Review of EQL method ordering: 2026-10-13

The user requested careful consideration before approving the CLOS correction.  The runtime source remains unchanged.

The [Common Lisp method-ordering rules](https://www.lispworks.com/documentation/HyperSpec/Body/07_ffab.htm) require comparison to continue when corresponding specializers agree.  Two EQL specializers in applicable methods necessarily agree, because each matched the same argument.  The [specializer agreement rules](https://www.lispworks.com/documentation/HyperSpec/Body/07_fc.htm) define that agreement by EQL on their objects.

Source review narrows the recommendation to METHOD-MORE-SPECIFIC-WITH-ARGS-P.  Its sole caller, STD-COMPUTE-APPLICABLE-METHODS, first filters methods against the supplied arguments.  In the matching-EQL branch, replace the early RETURN NIL with NIL so the loop compares the next argument.  The existing argument-reordering table already supplies the generic function's precedence order.  Class comparison and EQL-versus-class comparison remain unchanged.

The earlier recommendation also changed METHOD-MORE-SPECIFIC-P.  Its sole caller, STD-COMPUTE-APPLICABLE-METHODS-USING-CLASSES, rejects an EQL specializer before sorting.  No EQL-specialized method reaches that comparator through this caller, so that edit is unnecessary and is withdrawn.

The offline profile verified that captured certificate chain using the existing trust roots and server-auth purpose.  Of 934 samples under certificate verification, 789 included %%BIGNUM-TRUNCATE.  Allocation, integer shifts, addition, and canonicalization accounted for most direct samples.  Evidence is home/web-https-profile.txt and web-https-profile-run.log in the build directory.  Source review confirms that %%BIGNUM-TRUNCATE implements bit-at-a-time division with fresh integer results for its shifts, subtraction, and quotient accumulation.

The recommendation is to replace this division routine with normalized long division over groups of bits, using bounded integer arithmetic and temporary digit arrays in Lisp.  [GMP's basecase-division description](https://gmplib.org/manual/Basecase-Division) identifies the standard algorithm as Knuth section 4.3.1, Algorithm D.  The implementation must preserve Common Lisp quotient and remainder signs, division-by-zero behavior, and its input integers.  No dependencies are proposed.  This changes shared arithmetic and requires user approval under the latest design-decision rule.

Tests must compare results against SBCL across signs, zero and unit divisors, powers-of-two boundaries, exact division, short and long quotients, quotient-estimate corrections, and randomized operands.  Then measure the same offline certificate verification, test valid and rejected TLS connections, and repeat COMPLETE through headless Chromium with returned text visible.  Boot and snapshot restoration must pass before saving the arithmetic change.  The runtime source remains unchanged, and the browser failure remains unresolved.

## Standalone long division: 2026-10-12

The user requested the core long-division algorithm and benchmarks under Mezzano and SBCL, with SBCL running on the VM host.  This authorizes a standalone arithmetic experiment.  TLS requests and runtime arithmetic replacement remain deferred.

The implementation will use normalized long division with base 2^30 digits in Lisp arrays.  Two-digit estimates and digit products fit in the fixnums of both tested x86-64 implementations.  The public function will accept integers and return TRUNCATE's quotient and remainder.  Conversion to and from digit arrays will be included in the reported times.  The compiler policy will retain safety checks and specify the same speed and debug qualities in both implementations.  The algorithm follows [GMP's description of basecase division](https://gmplib.org/manual/Basecase-Division), which cites Knuth Algorithm D.

The simplest comparison uses identical deterministic operands on both systems and measures both the new function and each implementation's existing TRUNCATE.  Sizes will include 512/256 and 768/384 bits for elliptic-curve modular arithmetic and larger operands.  Tests will cover signs, zero, unit divisors, digit boundaries, normalization, quotient-estimate correction, add-back correction, and randomized cases.  Both returned values must agree with the existing implementation, satisfy the division identity, and meet the remainder bounds.

The standalone implementation passed 7,276 cases under SBCL and Mezzano.  The first benchmark used calibrated batches targeting 0.2 seconds.  Guest samples varied enough that calibration sometimes selected batches lasting only 0.1 seconds during measurement.  The benchmark now measures five samples of at least one second each, alternating the two functions.  This removes calibration and retains allocation, collection, and scheduling time in the wall-clock results.  The arithmetic code remains unchanged.

The one-second benchmark completed on the VM host under SBCL 2.5.2.debian and in the x86-64 KVM guest.  For 768/384-bit operands, medians were 1.828 microseconds for the new routine and 0.215 for TRUNCATE under SBCL, and 98.270 and 202.922 under Mezzano.  At 4096/2048 bits, Mezzano's new routine was slower: 5312.500 versus 3039.773 microseconds.  Guest sample ranges were broad.  The [long-division report](../long-division.md) records all sizes, ranges, compiler policies, and reproduction commands.

The user requested confirmation that the code was compiled and suggested a fresh Lisp process.  Both benchmark runs had loaded compiled files.  A fresh SBCL process then loaded only the three FASLs, checked COMPILED-FUNCTION-P for every source-defined function, the generator closure, and TRUNCATE, and passed 7,276 cases.  Mezzano reloaded the three LLFs, passed the same compilation checks, and passed the same cases.  The guest remained running with its retained debugger jobs.  Evidence is long-division-sbcl-compiled.log and long-division-mezzano-compiled.log.

The results support keeping this as a standalone experiment.  Its certificate-sized division improves on Mezzano's current routine, but the same Lisp remains much slower in Mezzano than SBCL and its largest measured division is slower than Mezzano's existing routine.  Runtime integration and TLS tests remain deferred.

## Long-division loop isolation: 2026-10-12

The user approved isolating the division loop over preallocated arrays and inspecting the generated machine code. The extracted loop passed the existing 7,276 integer cases in both implementations.  The preallocated-array results matched TRUNCATE before and after timing.  At 768/384 bits, restoration plus division measured 16.259 microseconds in Mezzano and 0.698 in SBCL.  At 4096/2048 bits, it measured 351.124 and 15.373 microseconds.  Restoration alone measured 1.161 and 0.016 microseconds for the smaller case, and 5.917 and 0.054 for the larger case.  Both counters reported zero allocation during every timed array sample.

The integer wrapper allocated 56,940 bytes per 768/384-bit division and 5,533,784.5 per 4096/2048-bit division in Mezzano.  SBCL measured 3,070.4 and 45,041.2 bytes.  These counts cover 320 divisions per size.  Source review identifies repeated copying in input conversion: LDB calls ASH with increasing offsets, and %BIGNUM-RIGHT-SHIFT implements those offsets as successive 32-bit shifts, allocating a new integer each time.

Mezzano emitted 5,016 instruction bytes for DIVIDE-DIGITS, versus SBCL's 1,059.  Its 4096/2048-bit sample profile places 790 of 1,013 samples within DIVIDE-DIGITS in array-access functions.  The compiler probes show that AREF with an index of (+ I J) calls AREF-1, while (THE FIXNUM (+ I J)) lets the existing transform emit a direct load with type and bounds checks.  Both probes use safety 3.  File-level and local optimization declarations both selected speed 3, safety 3, debug 1.  The [long-division report](../long-division.md#preallocated-division-loop) records ranges, source references, reproduction commands, and evidence paths.

The recommendation is to address arbitrary-distance bignum shifts before runtime integration of division, then review the checked array-access transforms.  A shift can determine the whole-word offset and residual bit count once and construct its result in one pass.  The current 32-bit repetition creates unnecessary intermediate integers.  Such a runtime change requires separate approval and must preserve both shift directions, both integer signs, sign extension, zero and large counts, input immutability, and canonical integer results.  Compare against SBCL and remeasure this experiment before any TLS test.

## Long-division type declarations: 2026-10-12

The user approved adding declarations and measuring their effect.  Keep the algorithm, array representation, and speed 3, safety 3, debug 1 policy.  First declare inner-loop indices and computed array indices as fixnums.  Then add bounded types for arithmetic variables and digit reads.  Compare each stage with the recorded preallocated-array benchmark, retaining compiled output and disassemblies on both systems.

For each array access, J is between zero and SIZE-N, and I is between zero and N-1.  Thus J+I, J+N, J+N-1, and the guarded J+N-2 are valid nonnegative array indices.  N-1 and the guarded N-2 are valid divisor indices.  The loop counters can reach their termination values, so their declarations retain the full FIXNUM range.

Let B=2^30.  Digits and quotient estimates lie in [0,B-1].  NUMERATOR lies in [0,B^2-1].  A conservative BORROW bound of [0,B] gives PRODUCT at most B^2-B+1, preserving the unsigned-60-bit bound.  The next borrow is at most B, completing that induction.  DIFFERENCE lies in [-B,B-1].  CARRY is zero or one, and SUM is at most 2B-1.  REMAINDER is nonnegative and bounded by NUMERATOR initially.  Its correction increments occur only below B and leave it below 2B, so unsigned 60 bits suffice.  These bounds support declarations without changing arithmetic.

- [x] Test and measure declarations on array indices.
- [x] Test and measure bounded arithmetic declarations.
- [x] Inspect remaining calls and document the measured effects.

The index declarations passed 7,276 cases under both implementations.  Mezzano's restoration-plus-division medians improved from 16.259 to 9.467 microseconds at 768/384 bits and from 351.124 to 194.099 at 4096/2048 bits.  SBCL measured 0.697 and 15.297 microseconds.  Prepared results passed before and after timing.  The [report](../long-division.md#type-declarations) records ranges and evidence paths.

The bounded arithmetic declarations passed 7,276 cases under both implementations and a separate Mezzano build with *THE-CHECKS-TYPES* enabled.  The measured build used the default NIL setting.  Its LLF was reloaded after the checked test.  Mezzano's final medians were 9.438 and 194.099 microseconds, with overlapping ranges against the index-only version.  SBCL improved to 0.452 and 9.192 microseconds.  The final ratio is about 21 on both sizes.  Median allocation remained zero for the timed array operations.

The index declarations expanded Mezzano's division function from 5,016 to 9,591 instruction bytes while replacing general AREF calls with direct accesses.  Arithmetic declarations left its byte, instruction, and call-site counts unchanged.  SBCL's function shrank from 1,059 to 1,007 bytes.  A profile of 64,000 divisions places 262 of 1,163 samples within DIVIDE-DIGITS in ARRAY-DIMENSION and the other 901 in DIVIDE-DIGITS.  The [report](../long-division.md#compiler-costs-after-declarations) records the source explanation and measurement limits.

The next compiler recommendation is to specialize checked one-dimensional simple-array access with one bounds check.  Current transforms emit ARRAY-DIMENSION calls and two bounds checks at each of 16 element-access sites.  Preserve evaluation order, type and bounds errors, and SETF values, then test generated code and the same division benchmark. Shared runtime arithmetic and compiler source remain unchanged.

## Certificate verification target: 2026-10-12

The user asked whether modest compiler improvements could reach 100 milliseconds, and requested evaluation of native arithmetic or TLS alternatives.  This is an investigation.  No new dependency or runtime implementation is authorized by this discussion.

Exported the previously captured public chain and trust roots from WEB-HTTPS-DIAGNOSTIC into home/certificate-benchmark-data.sexp.  The file contains three DER certificates, 150 DER trust roots, and the guest universal time.  It contains no credentials.  A compiled SBCL driver parsed those inputs once, then called PURE-TLS::VERIFY-CERTIFICATE-CHAIN seven times with the captured time and :PURPOSE :SERVER-AUTH.  The verifier and P-384 source files have identical SHA-256 hashes in the host and guest source installations.  This Linux run follows the Lisp verifier path.

| Call | Wall milliseconds | CPU milliseconds | Allocated bytes |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| First | 60.006 | 60.808 | 43,829,872 |
| Second | 28.002 | 26.855 | 29,021,328 |
| Third | 24.002 | 22.478 | 29,021,120 |
| Fourth | 16.002 | 17.112 | 29,021,584 |
| Fifth | 20.001 | 20.319 | 29,021,792 |
| Sixth | 20.002 | 20.200 | 29,020,608 |
| Seventh | 16.001 | 16.970 | 29,053,856 |

Every call succeeded.  The six subsequent calls have a median wall time of approximately 20 milliseconds.  Evidence is certificate-benchmark-sbcl.lisp, certificate-benchmark-sbcl.fasl, and certificate-benchmark-sbcl.log in the build directory.  SBCL is 2.5.2.debian on the same Ryzen host.  Timing includes allocations and any collection during verification.  Library loading, certificate parsing, hostname verification, network communication, and TLS key exchange are outside the measured interval.  The first row is the first call after loading and parsing, rather than a cold-machine measurement.  The guest's earlier approximately 11.5-second trace measured this chain verifier.  These results establish that the Lisp verification stack can meet the requested arithmetic latency on this hardware.

The original Mezzano profile has all 934 verification samples under certificate-signature verification, with 789 under bignum division.  Most samples are in P-384 verification.  The approximately 1.8-second residual calculated earlier assumes only division changes.  Multiplication, shifts, allocation, and normalization in that residual can also improve.

Source inspection identifies three runtime costs.  %%BIGNUM-TRUNCATE uses bit-at-a-time division.  %BIGNUM-LEFT-SHIFT and %BIGNUM-RIGHT-SHIFT perform repeated 32-bit shifts with intermediate allocations.  %%BIGNUM-MULTIPLY-UNSIGNED calls %%BIGNUM-MULTIPLY-STEP for each word product.  That x86-64 assembly primitive allocates a three-word bignum for its 128-bit result.  Reading a full-width word can also allocate a boxed integer.  Compiler optimization of the standalone division loop addresses only part of this work.

The recommendation is to keep the TLS and certificate policy in Lisp and improve the arithmetic primitives through Mezzano's existing Lisp assembler.  Whole-word division, single-pass shifts, and multiplication with register carries and one result allocation address the identified causes.  This is runtime arithmetic work beyond the measured array compiler change.  Meeting 100 milliseconds in Mezzano remains an untested target.  An initial increment should replace repeated-shift allocation and measure the same chain, followed by word multiplication and direct runtime division, with independent arithmetic tests before each installation.

[GMP's low-level interface](https://gmplib.org/manual/Low_002dlevel-Functions) offers word-array operations with caller-provided result storage.  It is the relevant interface if importing arithmetic.  The high-level interface introduces another integer representation and allocation policy.  [GMP's allocation documentation](https://gmplib.org/manual/Custom-Allocation) specifies default malloc/realloc/free use and restrictions on recovery from allocation failure.  No general C foreign-call facility or C-library loader was found in the reviewed Mezzano source.  A GMP port therefore requires calling-convention integration, GC-safe buffer ownership, linked-code loading, and defined error behavior.  Secret-dependent arithmetic also requires operation-specific analysis: GMP distinguishes its secure low-level functions from ordinary operations.

A second alternative is a native complete signature-verification operation.  [BearSSL's ECDSA API](https://bearssl.org/apidoc/bearssl__ec_8h.html) takes a digest, public key, and signature, and returns success or failure.  This boundary would keep certificate parsing, trust, hostname, validity, and chain rules in Lisp while avoiding repeated Lisp/C conversions for each integer operation.  Its [published measurements](https://bearssl.org/speed.html) show 360 P-384 point multiplications per second on a 2012 Xeon with generic i31 code.  Those results support feasibility, but do not measure our guest or a complete certificate check.  Library selection, native integration, and a same-chain benchmark require a separate approved implementation decision.

A complete native TLS port changes a larger part of the system than the measured arithmetic costs require.  The immediate choice is between improving the existing arithmetic primitives and importing a bounded native cryptographic operation.  General compiler development remains useful but has no measured basis for a 115-fold reduction of this complete workload by itself.

## Arithmetic runtime implementation: 2026-10-12

The user approved the existing-assembler arithmetic route and the certificate acceptance test.  Implement single-pass shifts, word multiplication without per-product allocation, and word division as separate increments.  Each increment requires independent integer results, input immutability, canonical results, collection tests, and the same captured-chain timing.  Build, boot, and snapshot restoration must cover the final installed source.  No dependency is added.

The first increment extends x86-64 %%BIGNUM-LEFT-SHIFT and %%BIGNUM-RIGHT-SHIFT to any nonnegative fixnum count.  Split the count into a whole-word offset and a residual count modulo 64.  Left shift fills the low words with zeros, copies shifted source words, and emits the final sign word.  Right shift skips whole words, joins adjacent words, and sign-extends the final word.  Counts that discard the complete input return its sign.  Zero count returns the input.  The existing canonicalizer retains its responsibility for shrinking and fixnum conversion.  The x86-64 wrappers call the extended routines once.  ARM64 retains its current implementation.

The ABI requires RBX and R8 through R13 to hold tagged values during collection.  Byte offsets in these registers remain multiples of eight.  Raw words and bit counts use RAX, RCX, RDX, RSI, and RDI.  The original input and count occupy marked stack slots across allocation.  Source pointers are reloaded after allocation.  The reference is [the register and GC metadata specification](../../doc/internals/abi.md).

The shift candidates passed 4,278 SBCL-generated cases before installation.  The installed routines passed those cases and all 7,276 division cases.  The first version of the test had a missing closing parenthesis, which SBCL rejected before guest compilation.  Its first input-immutability test used decimal formatting.  The revised fixture includes a separate copy of the original integer so comparison avoids formatting overhead.

The existing certificate chain verified in 12.31 and 8.99 seconds before installation, allocating approximately 7.908 GB each time.  After installation, three calls took 8.71, 8.24, and 8.30 seconds and allocated approximately 7.659 GB each.  Evidence is bignum-shift-installed.log.  This sample variation does not support a precise speedup ratio.  The reusable compiled benchmark is tools/https/bench-certificates.lisp.  Multiplication and division remain pending.

Four full collections forced at the bignum allocation call passed for positive and negative inputs shifted left and right.  The test temporarily replaces the allocator with a thread-selected wrapper, disarms before collecting, and restores the original function with UNWIND-PROTECT.  Evidence is bignum-shift-gc.log.  Fresh-image boot and snapshot restoration will test the combined arithmetic source after the remaining increments.

The multiplication increment keeps the signed Lisp wrapper and replaces the x86-64 unsigned word loop.  A single work bignum holds the result.  Each MUL produces RDX:RAX, and ADD/ADC incorporates the existing destination word and carry.  No word product becomes a Lisp integer.  The existing canonicalizer handles the final representation.  Inputs and byte indices occupy GC-visible registers, and raw words and carries use the unscanned registers.  Compare a separate candidate with SBCL before installing it, then run the division cases, force collection at allocation, and repeat the certificate benchmark.

The unsigned multiplication candidate and the installed signed operation passed 1,824 SBCL-generated cases with immutability and representation checks.  Two full collections at allocation and all 7,276 division cases passed.  The certificate chain took 7.38, 7.21, and 7.21 seconds and allocated approximately 6.992 GB per call.  Evidence is bignum-multiply-sbcl.log, bignum-multiply-candidate.log, and bignum-multiply-installed.log.  The x86-64 unsigned implementation is now in bignum-x86-64.lisp, while the previous Lisp loop remains selected on other architectures.

The division increment uses Algorithm D over normalized unsigned 32-bit digits.  A 64-bit multiply holds a complete digit product, and a 64-bit divide computes a two-digit quotient estimate.  This avoids 128-bit arithmetic in the inner loop.  The bignum representation remains unchanged.  Lisp handles signs, zero and unit divisors, sizes, and temporary allocation.  Assembly helpers copy and normalize digits, divide the three work arrays, and construct the quotient and remainder.  The digit loop performs no allocation.  Quotient correction and add-back follow the standalone algorithm with base 2^32 instead of 2^30.  The [basecase division description](https://gmplib.org/manual/Basecase-Division) is the algorithm reference.

Run SBCL-generated signed cases and explicit estimate/add-back examples before changing %%BIGNUM-TRUNCATE.  Compare the installed runtime with the standalone implementation, check zero-divisor conditions and collection across allocation, and repeat the same chain measurement.  The temporary buffers and helper interfaces are internal to x86-64 arithmetic.

The first assembly attempt used an IMUL form that the assembler interpreted as an immediate relocation.  The digit products now use unsigned MUL, matching their unsigned operands.  No failed candidate was installed.

## Proposed certificate-signature cache: 2026-10-12

The user proposed an in-memory cache with a 24-hour TTL and 1,024-entry limit.  Source review found separate certificate-signature and chain-policy functions in pure-tls.  VERIFY-CERTIFICATE-CHAIN checks dates, critical extensions, issuer relationships, CA/key-usage/path-length constraints, requested purpose, and the current trust roots.  VERIFY-CLIENT-PEER-CERTIFICATE performs hostname verification.  The previous profile identifies certificate-signature verification as the expensive operation.

The recommendation is to cache successful VERIFY-CERTIFICATE-SIGNATURE results.  A key must cover every verification input: signed certificate bytes, signature bytes, signature algorithm and parameters, and the issuer public-key algorithm, parameters, and bytes.  Use the existing SHA-256 implementation to keep keys bounded in size, with an unambiguous encoding of those fields.  Recompute keys from the fields the verifier reads, since parsed certificate structures are mutable.  Chain validation and hostname checks continue on every connection, including resumed sessions.  Configured revocation processing retains its existing path.  The per-handshake CertificateVerify signature remains checked against its current handshake transcript.

[Certificate processing in RFC 5280](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5280.html#section-6.1.3) separates signature verification from current-time validity and revocation checks.  [TLS CertificateVerify in RFC 8446](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8446.html#section-4.4.3) authenticates the handshake transcript.  These support placing this cache at the certificate-signature function.

Proposed mechanics: cache positive results for 86,400 elapsed seconds from verification, retain at most 1,024 entries, evict the oldest verification when full, and protect lookup/insertion with the existing Lisp threading library.  Verification runs outside the cache lock.  Cache use discards entries from an earlier Mezzano boot or snapshot restoration.  Loading revised cache/verifier code clears the cache.  Provide an explicit clear operation.  No new dependency is proposed.

Tests would cover hits and cryptographic call counts, expiration, capacity, changed signature inputs, failed verification, concurrent insertion, and boot invalidation.  Existing valid/expired, hostname, trust-store, purpose, chain-constraint, and resumed-session checks must behave the same with a warm cache.  Then measure the same public chain with cold and warm caches and run COMPLETE and CURL through Chromium.  This proposal changes which verification work a cache hit performs and awaits approval under the current design-decision rule.

The user approved implementation.  The cache will use fixed-size SHA-256 keys, an elapsed-time timestamp per success, and oldest-entry eviction.  The current parser represents EC public-key parameters in its algorithm value and RSA-PSS parameters in a plist.  The key covers those parsed representations.  Copy the signature inputs before hashing and verification so both operations use the same bytes.  A generation counter prevents an in-flight verification from inserting after a clear or boot change.  No new dependency is needed.

## EQUALP array hashing correction: 2026-10-12

The user approved correcting Mezzano and retaining the certificate cache's octet-vector keys.  Tests and compilation now run inside Mezzano.  The host uses the existing Python SWANK transport to submit guest Lisp, avoiding SBCL as a test runtime or client.

Source inspection: EQUALP compares vector lengths and active elements, and compares other array dimensions and row-major elements.  EQUALP-HASH omits arrays and numbers, sending both to its constant-zero fallback.  All distinct octet-vector cache keys therefore receive zero.  The cache's signature tests completed in the guest.  The earlier host client's 180-second wait expired during the storage tests.  That observation did not identify the unfinished operation, and the earlier deadlock speculation had no evidence.

Recommendation: add value-based numeric hashing and ordered array-content hashing to EQUALP-HASH.  Use one element-hashing path for specialized and general vectors, including strings, so equal representations receive equal hashes.  Vector hashing uses LENGTH and therefore respects fill pointers.  Other arrays include dimensions and row-major elements.  Preserve the existing depth bound.  Normalize finite floats through RATIONAL, matching the runtime's mixed rational/float equality.  Numeric zero, including complex values with a zero imaginary part, must hash consistently.  Handle infinities without calling RATIONAL.

HASH-TABLE already retains its construction-time hash function, but optimized lookup instead calls the current global function.  Use the recorded function in lookup.  Each table then retains the algorithm that placed its entries, including across code replacement and snapshots.  New tables capture the corrected function.  This uses the existing slot and requires no new table representation, registry, migration, or dependency.

Tests: cross-representation equality and hashes, case-insensitive character arrays, active vector lengths, displacement, dimensions, numeric representations, depth-limited circular arrays, insertion/lookup/removal and collection, distinct byte-key hashes, and survival of existing tables across replacement.  Compile and test a candidate first, then install it.  After the kernel increment passes, resume the cache tests, measure cold and warm chain checks, and test COMPLETE and CURL in Chromium.  Record observed results before claiming completion.

The candidate compiled inside Mezzano and passed 253 equality/hash comparisons across recursion depths, array representations, and numeric types.  All 1,024 distinct byte-vector test keys produced distinct hashes.  The installed kernel source compiled 154 functions in 201.69 seconds.  The host's initial wait expired before compilation finished.  Reattaching to its retained job retrieved the completed output and returned state.  No compilation was repeated.

The installed source passed 330 equality/hash comparisons, including negative numbers, signed zero, bignum/float equality, complex numbers, and cross-format infinities.  Array tests cover numeric specialization, general vectors, strings and character vectors, bit vectors, fill pointers, displacement, rank-zero arrays, dimensions, element order, and bounded circular hashing.  New-table insertion, equivalent-key lookup, replacement, removal, and all 1,024 byte-key lookups passed after full collection.  A table populated before function replacement remained readable after replacement and full collection.  Evidence: hash-candidate-test.log and hash-installed-test-final.log.

A separate attempt to collect a running worker's backtrace with ESTABLISH-THREAD-FOOTHOLD failed because its dispatch-manager wait item did not match an ETYPECASE branch.  Its own retained evaluation was aborted through its advertised ABORT restart.  That diagnostic produced no explanation for the earlier storage test.  It changed no hash-table code.

The certificate cache was recreated after kernel installation so its table captures the corrected hash function.  The cache tests, live HTTPS tests, and snapshot restoration remain to run.  No SBCL process compiled or tested this kernel change.  Guest Lisp was submitted through the existing Python SWANK transport.

## Expired Web session recovery: 2026-10-12

The user reported missing Listener SPACE completion, empty Document Examiner and Peek views, failed Listener evaluation, and HTTP 409 from /api.  Direct guest RPC tests returned completion, 16 documents, and 33 processes.  Fresh Chromium also completed those activities.  Those fresh-session tests did not establish behavior in the user's retained tab.

The Web agent reproduced every reported symptom by expiring its owned session, retaining sessionStorage, and reloading.  Resume returned HTTP 409 with :expired-session and uncertain=false.  The client left connected=false.  SPACE issued no assistance RPC, Return issued no evaluation RPC, Document Examiner remained empty after its rejected lookup, and Peek issued no process request.  The existing New Session menu action restored the functions.  Evidence: /tmp/ui-failure-repro/expired/.  The actual user tab remains unobserved, but this reproduces the reported HTTP status and failures.

Recommendation: use the existing New Session state transition after an explicit :expired-session response, at startup or during an active request.  Archive the former session's request records, preserve input and document history, obtain new guest tokens, and refresh the visible activity.  Prior evaluation and debugger requests retain their original identities and are never submitted through the replacement session.  Other errors retain their existing paths.  The correction adds no dependency.  The Web agent owns the implementation and browser tests.  Required tests include expired startup storage, active-tab expiration, successful completion/evaluation/documents/processes after recovery, preservation of pending records, absence of evaluation replay, and ordinary same-session reconnection.

The pre-restart saved image is mezzano-debugging-certificate-cache-saved.image with owner-only permissions.

Submission-to-observation times were 3,505, 5,413, and 10,958 milliseconds.  Those timings include network, endpoint execution, and browser polling, and do not measure certificate verification alone.  No debugger stop or application error occurred in these three evaluations. The certificate-cache implementation, expiration and capacity behavior, hashing prerequisite, TLS checks, snapshot invalidation, and stated fresh-browser evaluation tests passed on x86-64 Mezzano.  SBCL was used during the initial cache preparation before the user directed guest-only work.  All subsequent kernel compilation, cache testing, measurements, and restoration tests ran inside Mezzano through the existing Python transport.  ARM64, revocation, and the upstream TLS test suite remain untested.

## Concurrent job release during Web refresh: 2026-10-12

A fresh run of the broad Chromium driver received :released-job during initial refresh while another test cleaned up its own retained jobs.  The page remained connected, but refresh stopped and the driver could not observe its expected startup message.  The run created no evaluation jobs.  Evidence: /tmp/web-recovery-broad-results/failure-session.json and /tmp/web-recovery-broad.log.

The retained protocol lists global job handles, and the browser then reads each job's information and output in separate requests.  Another session can release a job between those reads.  Recommendation: a :released-job response while refreshing that particular job removes its local entry through the same operation used after a successful release, then continues the remaining jobs.  Other errors propagate.  This handles the guest's explicit deletion result and adds no retry, dependency, or protocol operation.  Test a live release before information and before output, preserving another completed job and Listener operation.

## Clear All: 2026-10-12

The user requested one command to delete retained evaluation and debugger clutter and then instructed implementation.  Clear All captures existing evaluation jobs, terminates running evaluation threads through the supervisor's existing termination operation, waits for their unwind cleanup, and releases their retained data and closed debugger stops.  The command excludes its own job and preserves submissions accepted after its target snapshot.  Its retained result supports original-request recovery.  The browser clears evaluation output, input history, and request/debugger records when that result returns.  Document Examiner viewers and bookmarks retain their documentation state.

Termination runs outside the service mutex.  Existing UNWIND-PROTECT cleanup closes debugger stops and evaluation streams.  Release retains used job names, preserving rejection of replay after deletion.  Active debugger stops in threads outside the evaluation service require separate debugger control and are reported.  Tests must cover a returned job, interactive input wait, an active debugger stop, unwind cleanup, new jobs after target capture, repeated command requests, and browser history after reload.

Tests cover typed offsets, Unicode output boundaries, stable handles, release omission, propagation of unrelated errors, and live Examiner startup, reading, and reload with retained jobs.

## Bounded monitor output: 2026-10-12

The initial monitor batch copied each retained job's complete unread output.  SWANK's six-digit hexadecimal message header permits at most 16,777,215 encoded bytes.  A sufficiently large retained output could therefore prevent the whole monitor response from returning, including the data needed to reach the Document Examiner.

Recommendation: share a 65,536-character output budget across unread jobs and use the existing ranged output reader.  Each row retains its full sampled output length and returns the start and end of the delivered prefix.  The next request supplies those end offsets.  Sort unread rows by supplied offset before distributing the remaining budget among the remaining rows, with at least one character while budget remains.  Short rows leave their unused share for later rows.  Response rows retain their original order.  This also permits progress when unread rows outnumber the budget without introducing a retained cursor.  The budget bounds output text.  Metadata and printed values remain subject to the protocol message-size limit.

Required tests: Unicode offsets and exact reconstructed output, invalid offsets, a growing output alongside a short job, more unread jobs than a small test budget, no handle growth, released-object omission, and Listener and Document Examiner responsiveness while output remains.  The browser must append partial prefixes and render a pathname presentation only after all its characters arrive.  A completed evaluation can still have unread output.

## Incremental output display and inline references: 2026-10-12

The bounded monitor reply requires the browser to accept a contiguous output range whose end precedes the sampled total.  Recommendation: append each range without dropping text, render a typed presentation only when its complete range has arrived, and display receiving-output progress beside the evaluation state until the browser reaches the sampled total.  This distinguishes completed execution from incomplete output transfer.  Tests must split Unicode text and presentation ranges across replies and reject invalid offsets.

Screenshot review of the passing Examiner suite found a layout defect in table references.  A wrapped native button occupies one atomic inline box, so its following period moves to a separate line even when the last text line has room.  An isolated Chromium measurement reproduced the period 16 pixels below the final reference character.  A non-atomic inline element placed both on the same baseline.  Evidence: /tmp/ui-quality-inline-diagnostic.json.

Recommendation: render inline topic presentations as focusable inline elements with button semantics and explicit Enter/Space activation.  Keep native buttons for candidate lists, bookmarks, headings, and menus.  Preserve the existing topic mouse operations and help.  Test wrapping with following punctuation, focus/keyboard activation, mouse reading, overview, and the topic menu, then repeat the Examiner interaction suite.  This changes no guest format, dependency, or documentation content.

## Document search Boolean conversion: 2026-10-12

The focused Examiner test found that Exact search with adjacency disabled reports Expected a Boolean.  The catalog represents Lisp NIL as JSON null.  The browser stored that value and sent it back to the HTTP API, whose typed adjacent argument requires true or false.  Existing tests had enabled adjacency and therefore missed the false case.

Recommendation: convert the catalog's null adjacency to false when creating browser search state and when constructing a search request from retained state.  Preserve supplied true and false values and leave unrelated invalid values to validation.  Test default/disabled adjacency across Exact, Initial, and Substring modes, including a retained null value.  This corrects the Boolean representation at the browser boundary without relaxing HTTP validation.

## Clear All concurrency and startup review: 2026-10-12

Source review identified three cancellation cases absent from the initial tests.  Concurrent clearing jobs can include each other in their target snapshots.  A termination delivered before RUN-EVALUATION-JOB establishes unwind cleanup can leave a dead job marked running.  A clearing command can overwrite package and pathname changes published by the preceding Listener job during its unwind.  The terminated count also counted requests whose target had already returned.

Recommendation: serialize clearing with one supervisor mutex acquired outside the service mutex, then capture job/thread pairs and stops under the service mutex.  Wait until a captured running job has established its interrupt-ready boundary, or has completed, before requesting termination outside that mutex.  Join captured threads without the service mutex and count the termination result returned by THREAD-JOIN.  Keep a waiting clearing job foothold-enabled so an earlier clear can terminate and join it.  A job accepted after the target snapshot survives that clear.  Skip package/pathname publication by the Clear All control command, which changes neither setting.

The reviewed mechanisms are WITH-SERVICE-LOCK in system/debugger-service.lisp, RUN-EVALUATION-JOB in system/evaluation-service.lisp, and the thread entry, foothold, join, and mutex implementations in supervisor/thread.lisp and supervisor/sync.lisp.  Required tests include cancellation before evaluation entry, a clearing job blocked on the clear mutex, normal completion before a termination request, Listener package/pathname preservation, existing unwind cleanup, and later submissions.  Global tests require a coordinated guest window because their purpose is to delete retained evaluations.

After the parser correction, all 11 malformed-input browser cases returned :invalid-private-document and left subsequent topic queries usable.  A missing unique pathname then reproduced HTTP 502 with uncertain=true from MEZZANO.FILE-SYSTEM:SIMPLE-FILE-ERROR.  Evidence: /tmp/document-commands-fourth/failure-api.json.  The test removed its owned file and released its 13 jobs and session.

Recommendation: classify FILE-ERROR and remaining STREAM-ERROR conditions during private-document reading as :private-document-io-error, retaining the requested pathname and original condition.  Reader syntax and truncated input remain :invalid-private-document through the inner handlers.  Missing topics retain :missing-topic.  Other condition types propagate.  Test a missing file through the browser and verify rejection, unchanged viewer, subsequent successful query, and cleanup.

## Saved-image startup failure: 2026-10-13

The snapshot completed, with :SNAPSHOT-COMPLETE returned and End snapshot in the serial log.  Restarting the saved image failed before SWANK initialization.  The serial trace reports WAKE-THREAD-1's sleeping-state assertion for cl-cancel-deadline-timer.  The caller chain is CONDITION-NOTIFY, BEAT-HEARTBEAT, PIT-IRQ-HANDLER, and INITIALIZE-PLATFORM-TIME.  The saved thread was already runnable or active when the condition notification attempted to wake it.  This is a kernel startup failure, not an HTTP failure.  The pending SWANK readiness probe reached its host deadline without a reply.

The failing disk is preserved as mezzano-debugging-ui-saved.image.  Preserve its serial trace separately.  Recommendation: restore a copy of the previously tested certificate-cache image as the guest environment for investigating and compiling a correction.  Reload the already tested UI backend from current sources there.  Determine the invalid thread/wait-queue transition in the snapshot, startup, and cl-cancel code before editing it, then test a new snapshot and restoration.  The earlier browser restoration test has not passed.

The cl-cancel review found native condition-variable use under the required mutex, with no library manipulation of thread state or wait queues.  An empty deadline heap still performs a one-second timed wait.  The Mezzano threading adapter delegates directly to native CONDITION-WAIT and CONDITION-NOTIFY, and these libraries have no restoration hooks.  The previously tested certificate-cache snapshot booted successfully.  Current UI backend modules are being recompiled and loaded in that diagnostic guest.

## Confirmed snapshot inconsistency: 2026-10-13

Read-only decoding of the failed image, using the image-manip.lisp format and instance layout, found the invalid state already on disk.  Thread 0x17b5be9 is cl-cancel-deadline-timer.  Its saved state is :RUNNABLE, queue-next is NIL, and queue-prev is run-queue predecessor 0x16d66f9.  Its saved wait-item is deadline-timer-condition at 0xe3fc29, whose saved head and tail both still point to that same thread.  The CV's wired page 0xe3f000 was copied before thread page 0x17b5000.  Boot consumes this contradictory snapshot rather than creating it.

SNAPSHOT-COPY-WIRED-AREA disables interrupts on its own CPU only.  Its existing FIXME records that stopping the world is insufficient to suspend the other CPUs.  Ordinary threads stop, but the other CPU's idle loop reenables interrupts.  A PIT timeout can therefore remove a CV waiter and make it runnable while those wired pages are being copied.  The saved older CV and newer thread match that sequence.

Required correction: quiesce interrupt handling on every other CPU for the wired-copy interval, using the existing CPU coordination mechanisms where possible.  Disabling a library timer or reducing QEMU's CPU count would leave the snapshot defect.  Review the rendezvous, lock ordering, and release path before implementing the exclusion.  Test a native timed CV waiter across a two-CPU snapshot, repeat restoration under timer activity, and complete the retained-browser and HTTPS checks.

The final UI backend recompiled and loaded in the diagnostic guest.  The guest Clear All suite passed again, including early entry, concurrent clearing, termination counts, input/debugger waits, cleanup, later submissions, and Listener defaults.  It released the old snapshot's retained evaluations as part of the explicitly global test.  Evidence: ui-backend-reload.log and ui-reload-clear-all.log.

## Snapshot CPU exclusion correction: 2026-10-13

Review of supervisor/x86-64/cpu.lisp and supervisor/thread.lisp supports reusing the existing TLB-shootdown rendezvous to suspend peer CPUs during wired copying.  BEGIN-TLB-SHOOTDOWN parks peers inside interrupt-disabled handlers.  The snapshot caller already holds the VM write lock and has stopped ordinary threads, which serializes this operation against page-table changes.  The snapshot worker cannot migrate while waiting because IRQ preemption excludes both the world-stopping thread and supervisor-priority threads.

Recommendation: on x86-64, begin that rendezvous before the existing local WITHOUT-INTERRUPTS copy, then call FINISH-TLB-SHOOTDOWN in UNWIND-PROTECT after restoring local interrupts.  BEGIN must run with local interrupts enabled: an in-flight BSP profiler may be waiting for this CPU's magic-button IPI acknowledgment.  Masking local interrupts before BEGIN could cause the two CPUs to wait for each other.  Peer CPUs remain suspended throughout copying.  Boot resets the saved shootdown-in-progress flag before enabling interrupts, and each rendezvous initializes its acknowledgment counter.

This correction uses the existing resident synchronization code and adds no library.  ARM64's corresponding helpers are no-ops, so the implementation and execution claim is x86-64 only.  Retain the scheduler's wake-state assertion.  Test the two-CPU image with a native timed-CV restoration fixture and existing timer activity, including saved queue/thread consistency and the original browser restoration checks.  The reader of the failed image is diagnostic evidence and does not modify it.

## Snapshot exclusion test: 2026-10-13

The scheduler has shared run queues and no inspected thread-affinity API.  A snapshot performed on the timer's BSP would mask PIT interrupts locally even before the correction, so a successful restore alone does not exercise peer exclusion.

Recommendation: install test-only wired observers around the existing BEGIN-TLB-SHOOTDOWN and FINISH-TLB-SHOOTDOWN functions, restricted to the snapshot thread.  Use preallocated wired storage to record copying CPU, BSP identity, PIT IRQ count, runtime counter, and TSC immediately after BEGIN returns and immediately before FINISH.  Require a non-BSP copying CPU, the rendezvous active with zero outstanding acknowledgments, unchanged PIT/runtime counts, and elapsed TSC longer than a PIT interval.  Keep observers free of allocation and printing and restore the original functions after measurement.  Bounded attempts may find a non-BSP run.  A BSP-only run does not establish that case.

The native CV fixture will establish two sleeping, queue-linked waiters with explicit long-lived timers, verify saved relationships before and after restoration, then test notification and timer expiration separately.  Each worker must reacquire its mutex and complete once.  Final assertions check empty queues, disarmed/unlinked timers, joined workers, and cleanup.  Pre-snapshot readiness is distinguished from the exact copying boundary.

The production wired-copy function compiled in Mezzano and loaded with wired allocation.  Its function address lies in the wired function area.  Disassembly places BEGIN before local interrupt masking and FINISH in the outer cleanup after flags restoration.  Evidence: snapshot-cpu-exclusion-load.log and home/snapshot-cpu-exclusion-20261013.disassembly.  No snapshot had run with the correction at this point.

TSC advanced 233696790 cycles at calibrated 2294843392 cycles/second, approximately 101.8 milliseconds.  Both endpoints reported an active rendezvous and zero outstanding peer acknowledgments.  CHECK passed.  The snapshot-completion reply and serial End snapshot marker both arrived.  Evidence: ui-snapshot-cpu-observed.log and ui-snapshot-cpu-check.log.

## Proposed COMPLETE keyword interface: 2026-10-13

Status: proposed for user review.  The implementation remains unchanged.

Source review: tools/https/listener.lisp accepts a prompt string and an optional positional model.  LISPM-OPENAI:CREATE-RESPONSE already accepts a Jzon request hash table and returns parsed JSON plus the HTTP request ID.  Reuse that transport and the private key file.  Keep the default model gpt-4.1-nano, store false, and one synchronous POST.

Recommendation:

    (complete input &key (model "gpt-4.1-nano")
                         instructions max-output-tokens temperature top-p
                         reasoning-effort verbosity text-format
                         (timeout 60))

INPUT accepts a prompt string or a nonempty proper list of message property lists, each containing exactly :ROLE and :CONTENT.  Roles are :SYSTEM, :DEVELOPER, :USER, or :ASSISTANT.  Content is a string.  Preserve message order and caller-owned data.  A native vector of Jzon input-item objects also permits caller-managed Responses history and structured content without another recursive conversion format.  Validate the vector and object types locally.  The service validates the item schemas.

MODEL is a nonempty string.  INSTRUCTIONS is a string.  MAX-OUTPUT-TOKENS maps to max_output_tokens, including reasoning tokens, with the documented integer minimum of 16.  TEMPERATURE accepts finite numbers from 0 through 2.  TOP-P accepts finite numbers from 0 through 1.  REASONING-EFFORT maps a documented keyword to reasoning.effort.  VERBOSITY accepts :LOW, :MEDIUM, or :HIGH and maps to text.verbosity.  TEXT-FORMAT is a Jzon hash table for text.format, including JSON Schema configuration.  TIMEOUT is a finite positive duration in seconds using the existing cancellation semantics.

NIL omits an optional API field.  Explicit numeric zero is sent.  Validate arguments before reading the credential or sending a request.  Model-specific API rejections propagate with the existing HTTP condition.  Keep the selected model and requested options on errors.  Replacing the positional model with :MODEL is a proposed source incompatibility.  Update repository callers, tests, and documentation after approval.

Return three values on success: concatenated output text, the parsed response hash table, and the HTTP request ID.  Structured JSON output remains a string in the first value.  The caller can parse it with Jzon.  Retain completed-status checking.  Add a completion condition for incomplete generation, refusal, and missing output text, retaining the response, request ID, reason, and any partial text.  Preserve existing transport and HTTP conditions.  The existing lower-level API continues to serve callers needing arbitrary request fields.  Streaming and tool execution require separate interfaces.

References: [Responses request reference](https://developers.openai.com/api/reference/python/resources/responses/methods/create), [text generation and message roles](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/text), and [structured outputs](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/structured-outputs).  The direct documentation fetch returned a placeholder for the generic request reference.  The Python reference and its linked text/reasoning schemas supplied the field definitions.

Required tests after approval:

- [ ] Compile and load the implementation and tests in Mezzano.
- [ ] Test message normalization, field encoding, NIL omission, numeric zero, validation, and preservation of caller-owned data.
- [ ] Test ordered text extraction, response/request-ID values, incomplete output, refusal, and missing text with response fixtures.
- [ ] Test prompt and message-history requests, sampling and output limits, schema output, and model rejection through the Web Listener with headless Chromium.
- [ ] Update the HTTPS reference and Document Examiner documentation, then commit and push the tested implementation.

## Tool-call design review: 2026-10-13

Status: recommendations for review.  Runtime implementation remains unchanged.

The initial tool proposal combined an execution loop with claims about recovery that require additional design.  Preserve the existing CREATE-RESPONSE transport and make COMPLETE own a bounded synchronous loop over explicitly supplied Lisp tool objects.  Tools contain a name, description, Jzon argument schema, and function object.  Keep exact name lookup, strict true, and parallel_tool_calls false.  Reject duplicate registration names before sending the first request.  Each callback accepts one parsed JSON object and supplies a Jzon-compatible result.  Application argument checks remain the callback's responsibility.  OpenAI strict mode requires additionalProperties false on every schema object and all properties listed as required, with nullable types for optional values.

Prefer :MAX-TOOL-CALLS, default 8, over the previous :MAX-TOOL-ROUNDS.  With at most one function call per response, N executed calls require at most N+1 model requests.  The last request may return final text.  A further requested call signals a limit condition before callback entry.  Validate response completion, supported output kinds, call count, nonempty call ID, registered name, and JSON-object arguments before invoking a callback.  Refusal and incomplete output signal conditions before tool dispatch.  Repeated call IDs signal a protocol condition before another invocation.  Separate calls with different IDs can still request the same operation.

Record callback entry, returned Lisp value, and encoded result as distinct stages.  A serialization error after the function returns must retain the returned value and original call.  A callback can commit a side effect and then signal an error.  Its effects are then uncertain.  A completed in-memory record cannot establish exactly-once external execution across failure or snapshot restoration.  The proposed initial increment performs no automatic request or callback retry.  Explicit resumption requires a separate API specifying run ownership, deadlines, and which stage may resume.

The Web debugger currently invokes restarts with zero arguments.  Source evidence: system/debugger-service.lisp invokes INVOKE-RESTART with only a restart object, and tools/swank/web/server.py accepts a restart index.  The proposed SUPPLY-TOOL-RESULT restart would require argument-taking or interactive restart support in that API.  Recommendation: retain original tool conditions and their live frames through ordinary signaling.  Support existing inspection and ABORT in this increment.  Keep argument-taking recovery as an explicit debugger feature rather than adding a tool-specific substitute.  HANDLER-CASE around the callback followed by a new ERROR would unwind the original frames and is unsuitable here.

One outer cancellation context must cover normalization, all HTTP requests, and callbacks.  CREATE-RESPONSE currently creates a context per POST.  Its library clamps child deadlines to the parent, so an outer context can provide the operation deadline.  Check cancellation before each request and callback.  Record a callback's returned value before the next cancellation check.  Expiration during a debugger stop continues to count toward that deadline.  Resuming execution after expiration signals cancellation before further requests or callbacks.  Cooperative cancellation cannot bound the return time of arbitrary callback code.

MAX-OUTPUT-TOKENS remains a per-request ceiling.  Under the sequential call limit, the generated-token ceiling is at most (N+1) times that ceiling, when supplied.  Input tokens and tool output sizes require separate accounting.  Report final text, final response, and final HTTP request ID as the first three values.  A fourth per-invocation run record retains ordered responses/request IDs, tool-call stages/results, and summed usage for responses received.  Usage excludes any server work whose reply was lost.  Credential strings and authorization headers are excluded from that record.  Original callback errors remain available to the retained debugger with the run on its live stack.  After unwinding, continued execution is outside the initial API.

Continuation must replay output items in order, including reasoning content and assistant phase, and append each function_call_output under its matching call_id.  Preserve the same instructions, model, schemas, and generation options on each request.  Keep intermediate assistant text in history and return the terminal response's text as COMPLETE's first value.  Caller-owned input and schemas must remain unchanged.

References: [function-call handling](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/function-calling#handling-function-calls), [strict schemas](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/function-calling#strict-mode), [parallel-call controls](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/function-calling#parallel-function-calling), and [reasoning continuation without stored responses](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/reasoning#preserve-reasoning-without-stored-responses).  Local review used the current client and debugger sources and the debugger reference.  A first source search used an incorrect debugger path; the implementation is system/debugger-service.lisp.

Tests required before claiming support:

- [ ] Counter tool followed by JSON encoding failure and by continuation HTTP failure: retain the correct call stage and preserve the counter value.
- [ ] Tool side effect followed by a Lisp error: original frames and condition remain inspectable, with ABORT available.
- [ ] Incomplete response, refusal, malformed arguments, unknown name, duplicate ID, unexpected multiple calls, and exhausted call limit: reject before callback entry.
- [ ] Zero-call limit, final request after the last permitted call, and per-request token limits.
- [ ] Expiration during a tool or debugger stop: retain completed work and check cancellation before another operation.
- [ ] Continuation carries instructions, call IDs, output order, reasoning items, and schema definitions without mutating caller data.
- [ ] Guest and headless Chromium execution with final values and intermediate usage/history inspection.

## Approved single-request COMPLETE implementation: 2026-10-13

The user restricted this work to COMPLETE and approved the single-request design.  That scope supersedes the execution-loop and recovery proposals above.  COMPLETE sends one synchronous Responses request, accepts tool definitions as JSON data, and returns requested function calls for the caller to handle.

Request construction accepts prompt strings, text-message property lists, and native Jzon input vectors.  Generation controls map to the documented request fields.  Tool selection accepts :AUTO, :NONE, :REQUIRED, or a function name.  Parallel calls default to JSON false.  The four values are text, parsed response, HTTP request ID, and ordered function-call property lists with decoded argument objects.  Tool-only output returns NIL text.  The model argument now uses :MODEL.  Updated its existing test caller and product examples.

COMPLETION-ERROR retains a reason, full response, request ID, and an argument-decoding cause when present.  Incomplete output, refusal, malformed output/calls, and absent text/calls signal this condition.  HTTP and transport conditions retain their existing behavior.  Request construction and response decoding have in-memory tests.  The implementation uses already loaded Alexandria, float-features, and Jzon.

## HTTP trace breakpoint and connection registration: 2026-10-13

The user requested a breakpoint for a subsequent COMPLETE call.  Installed `(trace :break t drakma::default-port)` in the running guest.  Drakma calls DEFAULT-PORT from HTTP-REQUEST before opening the connection for the Responses URL.  Its argument is the URI.  TRACE :BREAK uses Mezzano's existing function wrapper and BREAK support.  `(untrace drakma::default-port)` removes this breakpoint.  The request deadline continues during a stop, so the demonstration should use `:timeout 600`.

The initial installation check incorrectly expected FDEFINITION to return the trace wrapper.  FDEFINITION returns its original function.  Inspection through FUNCTION-REFERENCE-FUNCTION confirmed the installed wrapper and its true break condition.  Aborted and released the failed assertion job.

A headless Chromium evaluation of DEFAULT-PORT reached the retained debugger.  An inspection request then returned HTTP 409 with :CLOSED-CONNECTION.  SWANK's MAKE-CONNECTION queues :ADD-CONNECTION to its sentinel and returns before that message must be handled.  The caller can start request workers while the connection is absent from SWANK::*CONNECTIONS*.  The retained service's CHECK-CONNECTION tests membership in that list and rejects an absent connection as closed.  This ordering permits the observed rejection.  Recommendation: complete connection registration before starting request workers, preserving the sentinel's ownership of the connection list.  This correction requires user approval and remains unimplemented.

Inspected the owned test stop through SWANK, confirmed the trace-break condition, invoked CONTINUE, and checked the returned value 443.  Released its stop, job, and session.  The breakpoint remains armed for the user's call.  Evidence: /tmp/http-breakpoint-web.log and the retained-stop inspection result.  The Web debugger test failed.  No source implementation changed in this increment.

## Connection registration correction: 2026-10-13

The user approved completing SWANK connection registration before starting request handling.  Keep the sentinel as the owner of SWANK::*CONNECTIONS*.  MAKE-CONNECTION will send its registration request and wait for a matching acknowledgment.  The sentinel adds the connection before acknowledging it.  Calls with no sentinel or from the sentinel register through SENTINEL-SERVE on the calling thread.  Use the existing SEND and RECEIVE-IF interfaces.  The change adds no dependency or retry behavior.

Test delayed registration with an isolated sentinel, direct registration, and registration from the sentinel thread.  Compile and load the changed functions in Mezzano.  Exercise repeated fresh SWANK connections and headless Chromium inspection and CONTINUE at the armed HTTP trace breakpoint.  Preserve the breakpoint for the user's COMPLETE call.  Inspect the resulting test state and remove only owned test jobs and stops.

Headless Chromium displayed the DEFAULT-PORT trace condition and restart list, and CONTINUE returned 443.  A full COMPLETE request then reached the same stop, displayed HTTP-REQUEST and COMPLETE frames, continued, and returned the requested text.  The first full-request browser test incorrectly read an information field from the browser's stop record and raised a JavaScript TypeError after continuing the first stop.  Its cleanup script then tried to iterate a NIL stop list.  Corrected both test assumptions, confirmed that request's completion, and released its owned stops, job, and session.  The corrected browser test passed with one trace stop and zero browser errors.  It released all its owned records.  Evidence: /tmp/http-breakpoint-web-fixed.log, /tmp/complete-breakpoint-web.log, and /tmp/complete-breakpoint-web-fixed.log.

## Nested debugger abort and interactive restarts: 2026-10-13

Implemented a per-command ABORT restart around execution and unwind cleanup.  Typed :Abort resolves the innermost CL:ABORT object captured by the selected stop, so the operation's own new restart cannot shadow that selection.  Aborting a child operation records the parent's command as :UNWOUND and returns to its command loop.  Added guest :Help and indexed INVOKE-RESTART-INTERACTIVELY.  The original zero-argument operation remains available.  Listener information now samples its own active job's innermost stop, depth, and nesting prompt under the service lock.  SWANK encodes that stop using its existing token table.

## Interrupting a debugger operation: 2026-10-13

The existing delivery check cancels an interrupt whenever *SERVICE-CURRENT-STOP* is non-NIL.  Consequently a blocked condition printer or interactive restart reader cannot enter a child debugger.  A second request also fails while the original evaluation interrupt remains running.  The approved specification requires recovery from both cases.

Use the existing numbered interrupt requests and supervisor footholds.  Bind a command marker only inside the command's established ABORT restart.  Delivery at a retained stop requires that marker to identify the stop's active command.  A parked command loop still receives :ALREADY-STOPPED.  A nested request captures the preceding running interrupt in its delivery closure.  Finishing or cancelling the nested request restores that preceding record, preserving its result and replay identity.  This requires no new structure layout or separate interruption service.  Job information will report :INTERRUPTIBLE-P from the current command and interrupt state for browser button availability.  Delivery rechecks state on the target thread and respects WITHOUT-FOOTHOLDS.

Tests must interrupt condition printing, an interactive argument read, and a command inside an earlier interruption stop.  They must continue the interrupted operation or abort only that operation, preserve the original stop, reject a second pending request, retain identical-ID replay, restore the preceding interrupt ID, execute cleanup once, and permit subsequent evaluation.  Existing evaluation-interruption tests remain required.  Browser tests must use Interrupt, the resulting child stop, Abort, and continuation.  No live reload will occur during the Web agent's current suite.
